Before we jump in, a quick question to gauge where you're at:

**Do you know what Zod is and why you'd use it instead of (or alongside) the manual validation you already have in your register/login controllers?**

> AGENT

Great question. Let me break it down.

## What you have now

Right now in your register/login controllers, you probably have manual checks like:

```
if (!username) return res.status(400)...
if (password.length < 8) return res.status(400)...
```

This works, but it gets messy fast — especially when you have multiple routes that all need validation, and each one has its own pile of `if` statements.

## What Zod does

Zod is a **schema validation library**. You define a "shape" — what valid data looks like — and then you ask Zod to check incoming data against that shape. If it doesn't match, Zod gives you structured error messages automatically.

Think of it like a bouncer at a door with a checklist:
- Username? Must be a string, not empty.
- Password? Must be a string, at least 8 characters.

You define the checklist once, and Zod enforces it everywhere you use it.

## Why bother?

Three big wins:

1. **DRY** — define validation rules once, reuse across routes
2. **Consistent errors** — Zod generates clear error messages in a standard format
3. **Separation of concerns** — your controller focuses on *what to do*, not *is this data valid?*

## How it fits your project

You'd create schemas for:
- **Register** — username, password rules
- **Login** — similar shape
- **Favorites** — entity_id, entity_type, etc.

Then in each controller, instead of a pile of `if` checks, you parse `req.body` through the schema. Valid? Continue. Invalid? Send back the errors.

---

Does that make sense so far? If so, here's your first step: **go install Zod** (`npm install zod`) and then we'll look at your existing register controller together to see what validation you already have that we'll replace.

```javascript
export function register(req, res, next) {
  const { username, password } = req.body;

  if (!username) {
    return res.status(400).json({ message: "username is empty" });
  }
  if (password.length < 8) {
    return res.status(400).json({ message: "password is too short" });
  }
  bcrypt
    .hash(password, 10)
    .then((hash) => {
      return db.sql`INSERT INTO users (username, password_hash) VALUES (${username}, ${hash}) RETURNING *`;
    })
    .then((rows) => {
      res.status(201).json({ username: rows[0].username });
    })
    .catch((err) => {
      if (err.message.match(/unique|duplicate/i)) {
        return res.status(409).json({ message: "username already taken" });
      }
      next(err);
    });
}
```

**Route Definition:** The register handler is mounted in `/home/mohmqat/Work/week-5-project-express/src/router.js` at line 22:

```javascript
router.post("/api/auth/register", register);
```

**Summary:** The register handler validates the username and password, hashes the password using bcrypt, inserts the user into the database, and returns a 201 status with the username on success. It handles various error cases including missing username, short password, and duplicate username.

Sure, let's go step by step.

### Step 1 — Create the file

Create `src/validation.js`. At the top, you need to import `z` from the `"zod"` package.

**Question for you:** Do you know the import syntax your project uses — is it `import` (ES modules) or `require` (CommonJS)?

Look at the top of any file in `src/controllers/` if you're not sure.

### Step 2 — Define the schema

Once you have `z` imported, you'll use three things:

- **`z.object({ ... })`** — defines a schema for an object with specific fields
- **`z.string()`** — says "this field must be a string"
- **`.min(n)`** — says "this string must be at least n characters"

So the pattern looks like:

```
z.object({
  fieldName: z.string().min(someNumber),
})
```

For your register schema, you need two fields:
- `username` — a string, minimum 1 character (not empty)
- `password` — a string, minimum 8 characters

### Step 3 — Export it

Export the schema with a clear name like `registerSchema` so you can import it in your controller later.

Good effort — you've got the right idea! A few things to fix though:

### 1. The import

You don't need to destructure `string` from Zod. You just import `z` and then call methods on it:

```
import { z } from "zod";
```

Notice it's `{ z }` — it's a named export, not a default export.

### 2. Using `z.string()` instead of `string`

For each field, you call `z.string()` (it's a function call, not just the word `string`). And you need `.min()` to set minimum lengths. Remember:
- `username` — min 1 (not empty)
- `password` — min 8

### 3. Storing and exporting

Right now you're creating the schema but not storing it in a variable. You need to:
- Assign the `z.object(...)` to a `const` variable
- Then export that variable

> AGENT

Getting closer! Three small syntax issues:

### 1. Chaining methods

`z.string` and `.min()` need to be **chained** together, not separated. `z.string()` is a function call (needs parentheses), and `.min()` goes right after it with a dot:

```
z.string().min(1)
```

Think of it like a chain: `z` → `.string()` → `.min(1)` — each dot connects to the previous one.

### 2. Export syntax

In ES modules, you can't do `export variableName` on its own like that. You have two options:

- Put `export` in front of the `const` when you declare it: `export const ...`
- Or use `export { variableName }` at the bottom

Look at how your controllers do it — they use `export function ...`. Same idea, just with `const` instead of `function`.

> AGENT

That's correct! Your first Zod schema is done.

Now the next step — **using it in your register controller**. Here's how Zod works:

You call `schema.parse(data)` and it does one of two things:
- **Valid data** → returns the parsed data
- **Invalid data** → **throws an error** (a `ZodError`)

So in your `register` controller, you'd:

1. Import `registerschema` from `../validation.js`
2. Replace those two `if` checks with a call to `registerschema.parse(req.body)`
3. Catch the error if validation fails — a ZodError has a `.errors` array with the details

**Question:** Since your project uses `.then()/.catch()` and not async/await, and `parse()` is synchronous (it throws, not rejects) — how would you handle a thrown error inside your controller? Think about `try/catch` vs `.catch()` — which one catches synchronous throws?

> DEVELOPER

try/catch

> AGENT

Exactly right! `try/catch` handles synchronous throws, which is what `parse()` does.

So the plan for your register controller is:

1. Wrap the `parse()` call in a `try` block
2. If it throws, the `catch` block sends back a 400 response with the error details
3. If it passes, you continue with the bcrypt/database logic

**One more hint:** Inside the `catch`, the error object has a `.errors` property — it's an array of all the things that failed. You can send that back to the client so they know what went wrong.

Go ahead and update your `register` function in `src/controllers/auth.js`. You're replacing the two `if` blocks with a `try/catch` around `registerschema.parse(req.body)`.

> AGENT

Good attempt, but a few issues:

### 1. Missing import

You're calling `registerschema.parse()` but you never imported it. You need an import at the top from `../validation.js`.

### 2. `try/catch` structure

Your `catch` block is missing its curly braces `{ }` — and more importantly, the logic is in the wrong place. Think about it this way:

```
try {
  // parse here — if it fails, we jump to catch
} catch (err) {
  // send back 400 error and RETURN (stop here)
}

// if we get here, validation passed — do the bcrypt stuff
```

Right now your bcrypt logic is sitting inside the `catch`, which means it only runs when validation **fails** — that's the opposite of what you want!

The structure should be:

1. **`try`** — call `parse()`
2. **`catch`** — send `res.status(400).json(...)` with the error, and **return** so the function stops
3. **After the try/catch** — the bcrypt + database logic (only runs if parse succeeded)

### 3. The catch response

In the catch, send back the validation errors: `res.status(400).json({ errors: err.errors })`

Give it another go!

> AGENT

Two tests failed. This is a great learning moment. Look at what the tests expect:

```
expect(res.body.message).toMatch(/username/i)
```

The tests expect `res.body.message` — a **string** containing the word "username" or "password". But your Zod catch sends `res.body.errors` — an **array** of error objects.

**The response shape changed.** Your old code sent `{ message: "username is empty" }`, but now you're sending `{ errors: [...] }`.

You have two options:
1. Update the tests to match the new Zod error format
2. Transform the Zod error into the format the tests expect — a single `message` string

**Question for you:** Which approach makes more sense? Think about what the frontend might also be expecting when it gets a validation error back.

> DEVELOPER

i think updating the tests right ?

> AGENT

That's a reasonable choice! But let me push back a little first.

**Think about it from the frontend's perspective.** Your frontend code that handles register — does it read `res.body.message` to show errors to the user? If so, changing the response shape means you'd need to update the frontend too.

On the other hand, Zod's `.errors` array is actually richer — it tells you *which* field failed and *why*. That's more useful for a frontend that wants to show field-specific errors.

Either way works, but here's a middle-ground approach worth considering: **keep sending a `message` field**, but build it from the Zod error. Zod errors have a `.errors` array where each item has a `.message` string. You could grab the first one and send it as `{ message: ... }`.

That way your tests *and* frontend keep working without changes.

**What do you want to do?**
1. Update tests to match the new `errors` array format
2. Transform the Zod error into a `{ message: "..." }` string in the catch block

> DEVELOPER

transform zod is better right ?

> AGENT

Yes! That's the better call. Changing internal code is easier and safer than changing your API contract. Your tests and frontend already expect `{ message: "..." }` — keep that consistent.

So in your `catch` block, instead of sending `err.errors` directly, you need to pull out a useful message string from it.

**Hint:** Each item in `err.errors` has a `.message` property and a `.path` property (which tells you the field name). Try grabbing the first error's message:

```
err.errors[0].message
```

But here's the thing — Zod's default messages are generic, like `"String must contain at least 8 character(s)"`. It won't say "password" in the message.

**Question:** How could you make Zod include the field name in its error messages? Look at what `.min()` accepts as a second argument in the Zod docs. You can pass a custom message string there.

So you'd want to update both your schema (custom messages) and your catch block (send first error's message). Go for it!

> DEVELOPER

guide me

> AGENT

Sure, two things to change:

### 1. In `src/validation.js` — add custom messages

`.min()` accepts a second argument — a custom error message string. So instead of:

```
z.string().min(1)
```

You can do:

```
z.string().min(1, "your custom message here")
```

Write messages that match what your tests expect. Look at what the tests check for:

- `expect(res.body.message).toMatch(/username/i)` — the message must contain the word "username"
- `expect(res.body.message).toMatch(/password/i)` — the message must contain the word "password"

So your custom messages just need to include those words. Something like "username is ..." and "password is ...".

### 2. In `src/controllers/auth.js` — update the catch block

Change what you send from:

```
{ errors: err.errors }
```

to:

```
{ message: err.errors[0].message }
```

This grabs the first error's custom message and sends it as a string — exactly what the tests expect.

---

Two small changes, one in each file. Give it a try!

The schema and catch block both look great! But there's a syntax issue — look at line 13:

```
 }catch(err){
  return res.status(400).json({ message: err.errors[0].message })
 }}
```

You have an **extra `}`** on line 13. That closes the `register` function too early, which means the bcrypt code below ends up outside the function.

It should be just one `}` to close the `catch` block, not two. Remove the extra one.

> AGENT

Interesting — now it's a 500 error instead of 400. The key error message is:

```
TypeError: Cannot read properties of undefined (reading '0')
```

At `auth.js:12` — that's your `err.errors[0].message` line.

This means the caught error doesn't have an `.errors` property. That's because your `catch` is catching **all** errors, not just Zod errors. The Zod error object uses `.errors`, but maybe the property is named something different.

Let me give you a debugging hint. The Zod error class stores its issues in a property called `.issues`, not `.errors`.

**So:** change `err.errors[0].message` to `err.issues[0].message` in your catch block. Try that!

Almost perfect! One small thing on line 14:

`z.string("")` — the `""` inside `string()` isn't how it works. `z.string()` takes no arguments (or an options object for custom error messages). Just use `z.string().min(1)` without anything inside `string()`.

Other than that, the schema looks great — `z.enum`, `z.number()`, all correct.

Fix that and then wire it up in your favorites controller — same pattern as register/login: import, try/catch with `parse(req.body)`. Want to do both at once?

> AGENT

One test failing. The test expects `res.body.message` to match `/entity_type/i`, but Zod's default error message for `z.enum()` doesn't include the field name "entity_type".

Same problem you solved before with register — you need **custom error messages**. 

In your `favouriteSchema`, `z.enum()` accepts an options object as a second argument where you can set a custom message. The syntax is:

```
z.enum(["anime", "manga"], { message: "your message here" })
```

The test checks for the word "entity_type" in the message. Add a custom message that includes it. Give it a try!

All 27 tests passing! **Task 10a is complete** — you've got Zod schemas for all three: register, login, and favorites.

Let's update your progress and move on. The next item is **10b**:

> In addFavorite controller, fetch Jikan to verify entity_id exists and get the real title (don't trust user input)

**Question:** Why do you think this is important? What could go wrong if you just trust whatever `title` and `entity_id` the user sends in the request body?

> DEVELOPER

i mean user could ask for id 1000000 adn there is no anime in that id

> AGENT

Exactly! And they could also send a fake title — like `entity_id: 1` with `title: "something totally wrong"`. You can't trust client-side data.

So the idea is: when someone saves a favorite, your server should:

1. Take the `entity_id` and `entity_type` from the request
2. **Call the Jikan API** to check if that anime/manga actually exists
3. Use the **title from Jikan's response** (not from the user) to store in the database
4. If Jikan says it doesn't exist, return an error

**Question:** You already have a pattern in your codebase for fetching from Jikan. Where do your other controllers call the Jikan API? What does the URL look like for getting a single anime by ID?

1	export const animeDetails = (req, res) => {

3	  const { entityType } = req.params;
4	  if (!["anime", "manga"].includes(entityType)) {
5	    return res.status(400).json("Only manga and anime are allowed values");
6	  }
7	  fetch(`https://api.jikan.moe/v4/${entityType}/${id}`)
8	    .then((response) => response.json())
9	    .then((data) => res.json(data))
10	    .catch((error) => {

13	    });
14	};

> AGENT

Here's your existing pattern on line 7. It uses `fetch()` to call Jikan at:

```
https://api.jikan.moe/v4/${entityType}/${id}
```

Now in your `addFavorite` controller, after Zod validation passes, you need to do the same thing before inserting into the database:

1. **Call `fetch()`** with the Jikan URL using `entity_type` and `entity_id` from the request body
2. **Check the response** — if `response.ok` is `false`, that means the anime/manga doesn't exist. Return a 404 to the user.
3. **If it exists** — grab the title from the Jikan response (`data.data.title`) and use **that** as the title for your database INSERT, not the one the user sent.

Remember — your project uses `.then()` chains, no async/await. So you'd chain: `fetch().then().then()` before the database query.

Go ahead and try it in your `addFavorite` function. The fetch should go after the try/catch block, replacing the current `db.sql` query — or rather, wrapping it: fetch first, then if successful, insert into db.

### Step 2 — Check if the anime exists

Inside your `.then((data) => {` block, **before** the db.sql, you need to check if Jikan returned valid data. When an anime doesn't exist, Jikan returns a response where `data.data` is missing or the status is an error.

```
if (!data.data) {
  return res.status(404).json({ message: "anime/manga not found" });
}
```

### Step 3 — Use Jikan's title, not the user's

On line 26, you're still inserting `${title}` — that's from `req.body` (user input). Replace it with `${data.data.title}` to use the real title from Jikan.

### Also

Line 34 — the closing `}` for `addFavorite` should come after the `.catch`, not before line 36. You need a `.catch()` on the whole fetch chain too. Move the closing `}` after a `.catch()` at the end of the fetch chain.

Almost there! Two things:

### 1. Line 29 — still using user's title
You still have `${title}` on line 29. Change it to `${data.data.title}` — that's the whole point of this task, use Jikan's title instead of trusting the user.

### 2. Missing closing `}` for `addFavorite`
The function needs to be closed. After line 39 (the `)`), you need:
- A `.catch()` for the whole fetch chain (to handle network errors)
- Then a closing `}` for the `addFavorite` function

So after line 39 it should look like:

```
.catch((err) => {
  res.status(500).json({ message: "500 Internal Server Error" });
});
}
```

Fix those two things!

> DEVELOPER

but what about line 34-36

> AGENT

Good eye! You're right — there's already a `.catch()` on lines 34-36, but that one only catches errors from `db.sql`. 

Actually, let's think about this differently. You have two levels of promises here:

1. The **fetch chain** (outer) — fetch → json → data
2. The **db.sql chain** (inner, nested inside the fetch's `.then`)

The `.catch()` on lines 34-36 handles database errors. But what if the **fetch itself** fails (network error, Jikan is down)? Nothing catches that right now.

You have two options:
- Keep the inner `.catch()` for db errors AND add an outer `.catch()` after line 39 for fetch errors
- Or **return** the `db.sql` promise from the `.then()` callback, which lets a single outer `.catch()` handle both

The simpler approach for now: keep what you have (inner `.catch()` for db) and just add the outer `.catch()` and closing `}` after line 39. Go for it!

1	import { db } from "../db.js";
2	import { favouriteSchema } from "../validation.js";
3	export function getFavorites(req, res) {
4	  const username = req.user.username;
5	  db.sql`SELECT * FROM favorites WHERE user_id = (SELECT id FROM users WHERE username = ${username})`
6	    .then((rows) => {
7	      res.json(rows);
8	    })
9	    .catch((err) => {
10	      res.status(500).json({ message: "500 Internal Server Error" });
11	    });
12	}
13	export function addFavorite(req, res) {
14	  const { entity_id, entity_type, title } = req.body;
15	  try {
16	    favouriteSchema.parse(req.body);
17	  } catch (err) {
18	    return res.status(400).json({ message: err.issues[0].message });
19	  }
20	  fetch(`https://api.jikan.moe/v4/${entity_type}/${entity_id}`)
21	    .then((response) => response.json())
22	    .then((data) => {
23	      if (!data.data) {
24	        return res.status(404).json({ message: "anime/manga not found" });
25	      }
26	      const username = req.user.username;
27	      db.sql`INSERT INTO favorites (user_id, entity_id, entity_type, title)
28	VALUES ((SELECT id FROM users WHERE username = ${username}), ${entity_id}, ${entity_type},${data.data.title})
29	RETURNING *`
30	        .then((rows) => {
31	          res.status(201).json(rows[0]);
32	        })
33	        .catch((err) => {
34	          res.status(500).json({ message: "500 Internal Server Error" });
35	        });
36	    })
37	    .catch((err) => {
38	      res.status(500).json({ message: "500 Internal Server Error" });
39	    });
40	}
41	export function deleteFavorite(req, res) {
42	  const { id } = req.params;
43	  const username = req.user.username;
44	  db.sql`DELETE FROM favorites
45	WHERE id = ${id}
46	AND user_id = (SELECT id FROM users WHERE username = ${username})
47	RETURNING *`
48	    .then((rows) => {
49	      if (rows.length === 0) {
50	        return res.status(404).json({ message: "favorite not found" });
51	      }
52	      res.status(200).json({ message: "favorite deleted" });
53	    })
54	    .catch((err) => {
55	      res.status(500).json({ message: "500 Internal Server Error" });
56	    });
57	}
58

> AGENT

See the problem? The test on line 63-76 sends `{ entity_id: 20, entity_type: "anime", title: "Naruto" }` and expects to get back `title: "Naruto"`. But now your controller calls the real Jikan API and uses **Jikan's title** instead.

The tests don't mock `fetch` for favorites. You need to add a fetch mock so the tests don't hit the real API.

**But here's the question for you:** The test expects the title to be `"Naruto"`. If your controller now fetches the real title from Jikan, what should your mock return so the test still passes? Think about what `data.data.title` needs to be in the mock.

> DEVELOPER

explain that

> AGENT

OK. Let me explain what's happening step by step.

### Before your change
The test sends `title: "Naruto"` → your controller trusted it → stored `"Naruto"` in the database → returned `title: "Naruto"`. Test passed.

### After your change
The test sends `title: "Naruto"` → your controller **ignores it** → calls the real Jikan API to get the real title → but tests should never call external APIs → the fetch fails → 500 error → test fails.

### The fix
You need to **mock fetch** in the favorites test, just like the search test mocks it. The mock pretends to be Jikan and returns a fake response.

Since the test expects `title: "Naruto"` in the response, your mock needs to return an object that looks like a Jikan response where `data.data.title` is `"Naruto"`.

So the mock would return something shaped like:

```
{ data: { title: "Naruto" } }
```

That way: test sends request → controller calls fetch → fetch hits your mock (not real Jikan) → mock says `title: "Naruto"` → controller stores that → test sees `title: "Naruto"` → test passes.

**Question:** Do you remember how the search test mocks fetch? Look at your CLAUDE.md — it shows the pattern with `vi.stubGlobal("fetch", ...)`. Want to try adding it to the favorites test?

> AGENT

Here's the pattern. Look at lines 23-28:

```js
vi.stubGlobal("fetch", () =>
  Promise.resolve({
    ok: true,
    json: () => Promise.resolve(onePieceSearchStub),
  }),
);
```

It replaces the global `fetch` with a fake function that returns whatever you want.

For your favorites test, you need to do the same thing, but your fake response should look like a Jikan single-anime response — specifically, it needs `data.data.title` to be `"Naruto"`.

So the stub data would be: `{ data: { title: "Naruto" } }`

Now here's what you need to do in `favorites.test.js`:

1. Import `vi` and `afterEach` from vitest (line 1)
2. Add an `afterEach(() => { vi.restoreAllMocks(); })` to clean up
3. Add `vi.stubGlobal("fetch", ...)` **before** each test that calls `addFavorite` — that's the "saves the favorite" test and the "deletes a favorite" test (since it creates one first)

Give it a try! Start with just the "saves the favorite" test (line 63).

### 1. `afterEach` placement
Line 101 — it works, but it's cleaner to put it near the top with the `beforeEach` (around line 17). Not a bug, just style.

### 2. `vi.stubGlobal` on line 103
This is outside of any test — it's at the top level of the file. That means it would mock fetch for **every** test, including the login in `beforeEach`, which would break things.

You need to put `vi.stubGlobal` **inside** each specific test that needs it — right before the `return request(app)` call. Just like the search test does it on line 23.

So in the "saves the favorite" test (line 62), add the `vi.stubGlobal` **before** the `return`. And fill in the mock response:

```

  Promise.resolve({
    ok: true,
    json: () => Promise.resolve({ data: { title: "Naruto" } }),
  }),
);
```

Not quite — the `vi.stubGlobal` is wrapping the test on line 62, which breaks the structure. It needs to go **inside** the test function, before the `return`. Look at how the search test does it:

```js
test("some test name", () => {
    vi.stubGlobal("fetch", () =>        // <-- first: mock fetch
      Promise.resolve({
        ok: true,
        json: () => Promise.resolve(...),
      }),
    );
    return request(app)                  // <-- then: make the request
      .post(...)
});
```

So for your "saves the favorite" test, the structure should be:

```
test("saves the favorite and returns 201 with the created row", () => {
    vi.stubGlobal("fetch", () =>
      Promise.resolve({
        ok: true,
        json: () => Promise.resolve({ data: { title: "Naruto" } }),
      }),
    );
    return request(app)
      .post("/api/favorites")
      ...
});
```

The `vi.stubGlobal` is a **statement inside the test**, not wrapping it. Same thing for the delete test on line 88.

Try again!

Almost! The `vi.stubGlobal` is still mixed into the `return request(app)` on line 63. They need to be **two separate statements**. Think of it as two lines:

**Line A:** Set up the mock (this is its own complete statement, ending with `;`)
**Line B:** `return request(app)...` (the actual test request)

Right now on line 63 you have:
```
vi.stubGlobal("fetch",()=>{ return request(app)
```

The `vi.stubGlobal` is telling fetch what to return when called. It needs the full mock response — the same `Promise.resolve({ ok: true, json: () => ... })` pattern from the search test. Then after that's done, a **separate** `return request(app)` starts the test.

```
vi.stubGlobal("fetch", () =>
  Promise.resolve({
    ok: true,
    json: () => Promise.resolve({ data: { title: "Naruto" } }),
  }),
);
return request(app)
  .post(...)
```

Two separate things happening: first you set the trap (mock), then you run the test. Try again!

### Part 1 — The mock (a complete statement)

This tells JavaScript: "whenever anyone calls `fetch()`, return this fake response instead."

```
vi.stubGlobal("fetch", () =>
  Promise.resolve({

);

That's one statement, ending with `;`. It has nothing to do with `request(app)`.

```
return request(app)

```

### Part 3 — Close the test

```
});
```

So the full structure is:

```
test("saves the favorite...", () => {
    // Part 1: mock
    vi.stubGlobal(...);

    // Part 2: test
    return request(app).post(...).expect(...);
});
```

### 1. Lines 4-5 — Remove these imports
```
import { json, promise, trim } from "zod";

You don't need anything from Zod in the test file. `Promise` and `ok` are built-in JavaScript — not Zod imports.

### 2. Lines 68-70 — `Promise` and `ok`, not `promise` and `OK`
- `promise.resolve` should be `Promise.resolve` — capital P, it's a built-in JavaScript global
- `OK: true` should be `ok: true` — lowercase, it's a property name

### 3. Delete test (line 97) — still missing the mock
The delete test creates a favorite first (line 100-102), so it also needs the `vi.stubGlobal` before the `return`. Add the same mock there.

> DEVELOPER

Review — Week 6 (Database) + Week 7 (Auth)

Strong work on the learning objectives. Fundamentals are sound: parameterized queries via tagged templates, bcrypt hashing, JWT in an httpOnly cookie, protected routes via middleware, fresh-per-test DB, and mocking only external Jikan calls. Nice touch verifying entity_id against Jikan in addFavorite instead of trusting the client.
Security — most important to fix

    JWT never expires. src/controllers/auth.js — jwt.sign({ username }, secret) has no expiresIn. If the cookie leaks, it's valid forever.
    Cookie missing hardening flags. Sets only httpOnly. Add sameSite: "lax" (CSRF), secure: true in prod, and maxAge to match the JWT lifetime.
    Error middleware leaks internals. src/middleware/error.js returns error.toString() to the client — in prod this can expose stack traces, file paths, or raw DB errors. Return a generic message; log the detail server-side.
    No rate-limiting on /api/auth/login — out of scope for this week, but worth a TODO; brute-force is trivial otherwise.
    Minor: login uses generic "invalid credentials" (good), but /register returns 409 on duplicate → username enumeration. Acceptable tradeoff, just be aware.

Correctness / robustness

    JWT payload doesn't include user.id. Every favorites query re-runs (SELECT id FROM users WHERE username = ${username}) as a subquery. Put id in the token during login and use req.user.id directly — simpler, faster, one fewer lookup per request.
    addFavorite doesn't check response.ok from Jikan. If Jikan 500s, .json() may still resolve, and the only guard is !data.data. Also, a Jikan network error currently reports 500 to the client — it's a gateway issue, not an internal one.
    deleteFavorite doesn't validate :id. Non-numeric id → Postgres error → generic 500. A Zod coerce or a simple Number.isInteger guard fixes it.
    Unique-error detection via regex — err.message.match(/unique|duplicate/i) is fragile. Prefer err.code === \"23505\" (Postgres unique_violation). PGlite should expose the same code.
    register destructures req.body before validating. Low risk because express.json() gives {} on empty bodies, but move the destructure after parse().
    Controllers mix next(err) with direct res.status(500). favorites.js catches and responds inline, bypassing your errorMiddleware. Pick one pattern — forwarding to next(err) is cleaner and gives you a single place to format errors.

Schema (src/schema.sql)

    `user_id` / `entity_id` should be `NOT NULL`.
    `user_id INTEGER REFERENCES users(id)` should have `ON DELETE CASCADE` so deleting a user doesn't orphan favorites.
    No `UNIQUE (user_id, entity_id, entity_type)` — a user can save the same anime N times.
    No `created_at` on favorites (you have it on users).
    Formatting is inconsistent with the `users` block (lowercase `Check`, stray spaces, no newline at EOF).

Project conventions

    Naming inconsistency. `registerschema` / `loginschema` (all lowercase) vs `favouriteSchema` (camelCase) in `validation.js`. Also `favourite` (British) vs `favorites` (American) — pick one throughout; the table is `favorites`.
    CLAUDE.md says no async/await, but `tests/setup.js` uses `async/await`. If the mentor allows this in test infrastructure, add a note; otherwise convert to `.then()`.
    Route ordering in `router.js` interleaves auth, page-serving, API routes, and cache middleware with no blank lines. Group them (auth → page routes → cached API) for readability.
    Debug `console.log(data)` left in `public/favorites/app.js`.

Test coverage

The PGlite-per-test setup is excellent. Gaps to consider:

    `favorites.test.js`: no test for the Jikan 404 path (`data.data` missing → 404 to client).
    No route-level test verifying user A cannot delete user B's favorite via `/api/favorites/:id` (you tested this at the DB layer in `db.test.js` — lift it up).
    No test that `entity_id: "not-a-number"` gets rejected (Zod's `z.number()` won't coerce a JSON string).
    No test asserting the session cookie has `httpOnly`.
    The duplicate-username DB test uses `.then(throw) / .catch(assert)` — this also swallows assertion failures in the catch. `expect(...).rejects.toThrow()` is less trap-prone.

Nice things worth keeping

    Verifying `entity_id` against Jikan before INSERT.
    Mocking only `fetch`, never `db.sql`.
    Logging in via the real `/api/auth/login` to get a session cookie in `favorites.test.js` — better than stubbing middleware.
    Generic "invalid credentials" on login.
    Zod as a thin validation boundary.
    `db/init.sql` vs `src/schema.sql` split documented clearly in `docs/local/db.md`.

Priority fix list

    Add `expiresIn` to JWT + `maxAge`/`sameSite` to the cookie.
    Stop leaking `error.toString()` from `errorMiddleware`.

4	describe("users table", () => {

13	  test("each test gets a fresh database with only the seed row", () => {
14	    return db.sql`SELECT * FROM users`.then((rows) => {
15	      expect(rows).toHaveLength(1);
16	    });
17	  });
18	
19	  test("inserts a new user and makes it queryable", () => {
20	    return db.sql`INSERT INTO users (username, password_hash) VALUES (${"alice"}, ${"hash456"}) RETURNING *`
21	      .then((rows) => {

23	        return db.sql`SELECT * FROM users`;

27	      });

1	import { PGlite } from "@electric-sql/pglite";
2	import { db } from "../db.js";
3	import bcrypt from "bcrypt";
4	import { readFileSync } from "fs";
5	import { join } from "path";
6	import { beforeEach, afterEach } from "vitest";
7	process.env.JWT_SECRET = "test-secret";
8	
9	const schema = readFileSync(join(import.meta.dirname, "..", "schema.sql"), "utf-8");
10	
11	let pglite;
12	
13	function createPgliteSql(pg) {
14	  return function sql(strings, ...values) {
15	    let text = "";
16	    strings.forEach((str, i) => {
17	      text += str;
18	      if (i < values.length) text += `$${i + 1}`;
19	    });
20	    return pg.query(text, values).then((r) => r.rows);
21	  };
22	}
23	
24	beforeEach(async () => {
25	  pglite = new PGlite();
26	  db.sql = createPgliteSql(pglite);

1	import { db } from "../db.js";
2	import { favouriteSchema } from "../validation.js";
3	export function getFavorites(req, res) {
4	  const username = req.user.username;
5	  db.sql`SELECT * FROM favorites WHERE user_id = (SELECT id FROM users WHERE username = ${username})`
6	    .then((rows) => {
7	      res.json(rows);
8	    })
9	    .catch((err) => {
10	      res.status(500).json({ message: "500 Internal Server Error" });
11	    });
12	}
13	export function addFavorite(req, res) {
14	  const { entity_id, entity_type, title } = req.body;
15	  try {
16	    favouriteSchema.parse(req.body);
17	  } catch (err) {
18	    return res.status(400).json({ message: err.issues[0].message });
19	  }
20	  fetch(`https://api.jikan.moe/v4/${entity_type}/${entity_id}`)
21	    .then((response) => response.json())
22	    .then((data) => {
23	      if (!data.data) {
24	        return res.status(404).json({ message: "anime/manga not found" });
25	      }
26	      const username = req.user.username;
27	      db.sql`INSERT INTO favorites (user_id, entity_id, entity_type, title)
28	VALUES ((SELECT id FROM users WHERE username = ${username}), ${entity_id}, ${entity_type},${data.data.title})
29	RETURNING *`
30	        .then((rows) => {
31	          res.status(201).json(rows[0]);
32	        })
33	        .catch((err) => {
34	          res.status(500).json({ message: "500 Internal Server Error" });
35	        });
36	    })
37	    .catch((err) => {
38	      res.status(500).json({ message: "500 Internal Server Error" });
39	    });
40	}
41	export function deleteFavorite(req, res) {
42	  const { id } = req.params;
43	  const username = req.user.username;
44	  db.sql`DELETE FROM favorites
45	WHERE id = ${id}
46	AND user_id = (SELECT id FROM users WHERE username = ${username})
47	RETURNING *`
48	    .then((rows) => {
49	      if (rows.length === 0) {
50	        return res.status(404).json({ message: "favorite not found" });
51	      }
52	      res.status(200).json({ message: "favorite deleted" });
53	    })
54	    .catch((err) => {
55	      res.status(500).json({ message: "500 Internal Server Error" });
56	    });
57	}

> AGENT

All five P5 items are in place. Here's a summary:

**favorites.test.js** (3 new tests)
- `returns 400 when entity_id is not a number` — sends string `"not-a-number"`, Zod rejects (#23)
- `returns 404 when Jikan has no data for the entity_id` — mocks `{ data: null }`, expects the `!data.data` path (#21)
- `returns 404 when trying to delete another user's favorite` — inserts bob + bob's favorite via `db.sql`, then logs in as testuser and tries to delete (#22)

**auth.test.js** (1 new test)
- `session cookie is flagged HttpOnly` — finds the `token=` cookie in `set-cookie` and asserts `/HttpOnly/i` (#24)

**db.test.js** (refactor)
- `rejects a duplicate username` now uses `expect(...).rejects.toThrow(/unique|duplicate/i)` instead of the `.then(throw)/.catch(assert)` pattern (#25)

**Couldn't run the suite** — this environment has Node 18, vitest 4 needs Node 20+. Run `npm test` on your main dev box to verify.

```
1	import { LRUCache } from "lru-cache";
2	
3	const cache = new LRUCache({
4	  max: 1000,
5	  ttl: 1000 * 60 * 15,
6	});
7	
8	export const cacheMiddleware = (req, res, next) => {
9	  const cacheKey = `${req.method}:${req.url}`;
10	  // console.debug(cacheKey, "cached:", cache.has(cacheKey));
11	  if (cache.has(cacheKey)) {
12	    const { data } = cache.get(cacheKey);
13	    return res.json(data);
14	  }
15	
16	  // Imad note: This is monkey patching the `res.json` call, i dont know of easier ways to pick up the response.body, that means if you dont use res.json it does not get cached.
17	  const oldResJson = res.json;
18	
19	  res.json = function (data) {
20	    const time = Date.now();
21	    cache.set(cacheKey, { data, time });
22	
23	    return oldResJson.call(this, data);
24	  };
25	
26	  next();
27	};
28	
```

> AGENT

Structure is better. But one subtle issue: your page-serving routes (lines 23-29) are **after** `router.use(cacheMiddleware)`. 

Check `src/middleware/cache.js` — the middleware monkey-patches `res.json`. Page routes use `res.sendFile`, so they won't actually cache. But every request to them still runs the cache lookup needlessly.

The review suggested order was: **auth → page routes → cached API**. So page routes should sit *above* `router.use(cacheMiddleware)`, not below it.

**Question:** Move the two page-serving routes (`/search/:entityType` and `/:entityType/:id`) to sit between auth and `router.use(cacheMiddleware)`. Then add blank lines between each group so it's visually clear. Want to give that a shot?

> AGENT

Wait — this breaks caching! The public API routes (top, search, anime details) were the *reason* for the cache middleware (to avoid Jikan rate limits). You've now put them *above* `router.use(cacheMiddleware)`, so they bypass the cache entirely.

Think about it: the cache middleware only affects routes that come **after** it. So what you have now is:
- Auth ✓
- Favorites (user-specific, shouldn't be cached) ✓ above
- Top/search/details (public Jikan proxy, **should** be cached) ✗ above, bypassing cache
- `router.use(cacheMiddleware)` 
- Page routes ✓ below (doesn't matter, `res.sendFile` isn't cached anyway)

**Correct order:**
1. Auth
2. Favorites
3. Page routes
4. `router.use(cacheMiddleware)`
5. Top/search/details API (the cached stuff)

Move the three `/api/:entityType/...` routes (16-18) to sit **after** `router.use(cacheMiddleware)`, and page routes before it. Try again!

```
1	# Project: Anime Showcase & Search
2	
3	Express server serving 3 static HTML/JS/CSS pages, proxying data from the [Jikan API](https://docs.api.jikan.moe/).
4	
5	## Pages & Routes
6	
7	| Route        | File                       | Purpose           |
8	| ------------ | -------------------------- | ----------------- |
9	| `/`          | `public/index.html`        | Top anime home    |
10	| `/search/`   | `public/search/index.html` | Search by name    |
11	| `/anime/:id` | `public/detail/index.html` | Anime detail view |
12	
13	## File Structure
14	
15	```
16	public/
17	  index.html        ← top anime home page
18	  app.js
19	  style.css
20	  search/
21	    index.html
22	    app.js
23	    style.css
24	  detail/
25	    index.html
26	    app.js
27	    style.css
28	src/
29	  app.js            ← Express app, serves public/ via express.static
30	  server.js         ← starts server on port 9080
31	  router.js         ← API routes + manual route for /anime/:id
32	  db.js             ← exports db.sql tagged template (postgres.js in prod, PGlite in test)
33	  schema.sql        ← database schema (users table)
34	  controllers/
35	    top-anime.js
36	    anime-search.js
37	    anime-details.js
38	  tests/
39	    setup.js        ← global test setup (PGlite per test, schema, seed)
40	    search.test.js
41	    db.test.js
42	    stubs/
43	      search-one-piece.json
44	vitest.config.js    ← wires setup.js for all tests
45	```
46	
47	## Tech Constraints
48	
49	- **No async/await** — `.then()` / `.catch()` Promise chains only
50	- **No frontend framework** — vanilla HTML, CSS, JS
51	- **No build tools** — files served as static assets
52	
53	## Architecture
54	
55	**Proxy pattern**: frontend never calls Jikan directly. All API calls go through the Express server.
56	
57	| Express route            | Jikan endpoint            |
58	| ------------------------ | ------------------------- |
59	| `/api/top-anime`         | `GET /v4/top/anime`       |
60	| `/api/search?q=...`      | `GET /v4/anime?q={query}` |
61	| `/api/anime-details/:id` | `GET /v4/anime/{id}`      |
62	
63	- Routes use kebab-case
64	- Detail page URL: `/anime/:id` — Express serves `public/detail/index.html` via manual route in `router.js`
65	- Frontend reads anime ID from `window.location.pathname.split("/")[2]`
66	- Cards on home and search pages navigate to `/anime/{mal_id}`
67	- URL encoding via `encodeURIComponent` on search and detail pages
68	
69	## Jikan API
70	
71	Base URL: `https://api.jikan.moe/v4`
72	
73	Response shape:
74	
75	- Top-level keys: `pagination`, `data`
76	- Anime array at `response.data`
77	- Each anime: `title`, `score`, `mal_id`, `images.jpg.image_url`
78	
79	## Tests
80	
81	- Framework: Vitest + supertest
82	- Global setup: `src/tests/setup.js` (configured in `vitest.config.js`)
83	- Stubs in `src/tests/stubs/` (real Jikan response shapes)
84	- Test names describe user behavior, not implementation
85	- Each test validates: status code, content type, response body shape
86	
87	### Database in tests
88	
89	- Each test gets a fresh in-memory PostgreSQL via [PGlite](https://pglite.dev/)
90	- `src/tests/setup.js` handles `beforeEach`/`afterEach`: creates PGlite instance, runs `src/schema.sql`, seeds a test user (`testuser` / `hashed_password_123`), and swaps `db.sql` to a PGlite-backed wrapper
91	- Controllers use `db.sql` tagged template — same interface in prod (postgres.js) and test (PGlite wrapper)
92	- **Never mock `db.sql`** — queries run against real SQL in PGlite
93	- To add tables: update `src/schema.sql`, they'll be created automatically each test
94	
95	### Mocking external APIs
96	
97	- **Only mock external HTTP calls** (Jikan API), never database calls
98	- Mock pattern: `vi.stubGlobal("fetch", () => Promise.resolve({ ok: true, json: () => Promise.resolve(stub) }))`
99	- Import stubs: `import stub from "./stubs/search-one-piece.json" with { type: "json" }`
100	- No external API calls in tests — always mock fetch
101	
102	## Remaining Work
103	
104	- [ ] Caching on Express routes (avoid Jikan rate limits)
105	
```

- **Item 5** — `user_id` and `entity_id` are now `NOT NULL`
- **Item 6** — `ON DELETE CASCADE` on the foreign key (deleting a user auto-removes their favorites)

- **Item 9** — Consistent formatting: `CHECK` uppercase, proper indentation, no stray spaces, newline at EOF

1	# Review Follow-ups — Week 6+7
2	
3	Based on mentor review of database + auth work. Ordered by priority.
4	
5	## Priority 1 — Security (do first)
6	
7	- [ ] 1. Add `expiresIn` to `jwt.sign()` in `src/controllers/auth.js`
8	- [ ] 2. Harden the session cookie: add `sameSite: "lax"`, `secure` (prod only), and `maxAge` matching the JWT lifetime
9	- [ ] 3. Stop leaking `error.toString()` from `src/middleware/error.js` — send a generic message, log details server-side
10	- [ ] 4. Add a TODO comment on `/api/auth/login` noting that rate-limiting is missing (brute-force risk)
11	
12	## Priority 2 — Schema fixes (`src/schema.sql`)
13	
14	- [x] 5. Add `NOT NULL` to `user_id` and `entity_id` on the favorites table
15	- [x] 6. Change `user_id INTEGER REFERENCES users(id)` → add `ON DELETE CASCADE`
16	- [x] 7. Add `UNIQUE (user_id, entity_id, entity_type)` so a user can't save the same anime twice
17	- [x] 8. Add `created_at TIMESTAMP DEFAULT NOW()` on favorites (matches users)
18	- [x] 9. Fix formatting: lowercase `Check` → `CHECK`, remove stray spaces, add newline at EOF
19	
20	## Priority 3 — Correctness / robustness
21	
22	- [ ] 10. Include `user.id` in the JWT payload at login; use `req.user.id` in favorites queries to drop the `(SELECT id FROM users WHERE username = ...)` subquery
23	- [ ] 11. In `addFavorite`, check `response.ok` from Jikan before calling `.json()`; treat network errors as gateway errors (502), not internal (500)
24	- [ ] 12. Validate `:id` in `deleteFavorite` — use `z.coerce.number().int()` or `Number.isInteger` before hitting the DB
25	- [ ] 13. Replace `err.message.match(/unique|duplicate/i)` with `err.code === "23505"` (Postgres `unique_violation`)
26	- [ ] 14. In `register`, move the `const { username, password } = req.body` destructure **after** `registerschema.parse(req.body)`
27	- [ ] 15. Pick one error-handling pattern across controllers — prefer `next(err)` so `errorMiddleware` formats responses in one place (stop mixing direct `res.status(500)` in favorites.js)
28	
29	## Priority 4 — Naming / conventions
30	
31	- [x] 16. Rename `registerschema` → `registerSchema`, `loginschema` → `loginSchema`, `favouriteSchema` → `favoriteSchema` (consistent camelCase + American spelling to match `favorites` table)
32	- [x] 17. Update all imports that reference the old schema names
33	- [x] 18. Remove `console.log(data)` from `public/favorites/app.js`
34	- [x] 19. Group `src/router.js` sections with blank lines: auth → page routes → cached API
35	- [x] 20. Decide on `async/await` in `src/tests/setup.js` — CLAUDE.md now allows it in test infra
36	
37	## Priority 5 — Test coverage gaps
38	
39	- [x] 21. Test the Jikan 404 path in `favorites.test.js` — mock fetch to return `{ data: null }`, expect 404
40	- [x] 22. Add a route-level test: user A cannot delete user B's favorite via `DELETE /api/favorites/:id` (currently only tested at DB layer)
41	- [x] 23. Add a test that `entity_id: "not-a-number"` returns 400 (Zod's `z.number()` doesn't coerce)
42	- [x] 24. Add a test asserting the login response sets a cookie with `HttpOnly` flag
43	- [x] 25. Replace `.then(throw) / .catch(assert)` pattern in duplicate-username test with `expect(...).rejects.toThrow()`
44

69	## Understanding Checkpoints
70	
71	- Knows script tag goes at bottom of body (DOM must exist before JS runs)
72	- Knows `+=` vs `=` for innerHTML (preserves previous cards)
73	- Knows `.then()` chain — semicolon between chains breaks them
74	- Knows Jikan data lives at `data.data`, not at root
75	- Knows `createElement` + `appendChild` pattern for safe DOM manipulation
76	- Knows proxy pattern — frontend never calls external API directly
77	- Understands why mocking fetch matters (flaky tests, rate limits)
78	- Struggles with nested bracket/parenthesis structure in tests — needs more practice
79	- Understands URL as source of truth — `URLSearchParams`, `history.pushState`, reading params on load
80	- Understands `||` fallback pattern for default values
81	- Understands `document.querySelector` vs `getElementById`
82	- Understands native form GET behavior (no JS needed for URL navigation)
83	- Knows postgres.js tagged templates handle parameterized queries automatically (no manual `$1, $2`)
84	- Understands PGlite as in-memory PostgreSQL for test isolation (fresh DB per test)
85	- Knows to mock only external HTTP (Jikan), never database calls
86	- Writing tests before implementation (TDD approach)
87	- Understands user_id guard on DELETE — security pattern to prevent cross-user data access
88	- Understands entity_type validation (restrict to anime/manga)
89	- Understands Zod schema validation — `z.object()`, `z.string().min()`, `z.enum()`, `z.number()`
90	- Knows `schema.parse()` throws synchronously — use `try/catch`, not `.catch()`
91	- Knows Zod errors live in `.issues[0].message`, not `.errors`
92	- Understands custom error messages in Zod (`.min(n, "message")`, `z.enum([], { message })`)
93	- Understands why not to trust user input — server should verify against source of truth (Jikan API)
94	- Knows `vi.stubGlobal("fetch", ...)` goes inside individual tests, not at file level
95	- Knows `Promise.resolve` is a built-in JavaScript global (capital P), not a library import
96
